On a Local Existence Theorem for the Evolution Equation of Viscous Gaseous Stars in a Physical Vacuum
Demin Wang, Jiawen Zhang, Shengguo Zhu

Abstract
. This paper focuses on the free boundary problem of the three-dimensional compressible Navier–Stokes–Poisson equations with degenerate viscosities for self-gravitating viscous gaseous stars. For spherically symmetric barotropic motion, we establish the local well-posedness of classical solutions. The solutions obtained here are smooth all the way up to the moving boundary and capture the physical vacuum boundary behavior of the Lane-Emden star configuration for all adiabatic exponents γ > 4 3 \gamma>\frac{4}{3} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35A01, 35A09, 35R35, 35B65, 35Q30, 76N06, 76N10.

1.
Introduction

The motion of a self-gravitating viscous gaseous star in the universe can be described
by the following
vacuum free boundary problem (
VFBP
) of the three-dimensional (3-D) compressible Navier–Stokes–Poisson equations (
CNSP
):

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+\dive(\rho\bm{u})=0&\text{in }\Omega(t),\\[2.0pt]
(\rho\bm{u})_{t}+\dive(\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u})+\nabla P+\rho\nabla\Psi=\dive\mathbb{T}&\text{in }\Omega(t),\\[2.0pt]
\rho>0&\text{in }\Omega(t),\\[2.0pt]
\rho=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega(t),\\[2.0pt]
\mathcal{V}(\partial\Omega(t))=\bm{u}\cdot\bm{n}(t)&\text{on }\partial\Omega(t),\\[2.0pt]
(\rho,\bm{u})|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},\bm{u}_{0})&\text{in }\Omega:=\Omega(0).\end{cases}

Here,
t\geq 0
is the time,
\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the Eulerian spatial coordinate, the open and bounded subset
\Omega(t)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
denotes the changing volume occupied by the fluid and
\Omega(0)=B_{1}=\{\bm{x}:\,|\bm{x}|<1\}
,

\partial\Omega(t)
denotes the moving vacuum boundary,
\mathcal{V}(\partial\Omega(t))
denotes the normal velocity of
\partial\Omega(t)
, and
\bm{n}(t)
denotes the exterior unit normal vector to
\partial\Omega(t)
.
Moreover,
\rho\geq 0
denotes the mass density of the fluid,
\bm{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})^{\top}

\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
the Eulerian velocity field, and
P
the pressure.
For polytropic fluids, the constitutive relation is given by

(1.2)

P=A\rho^{\gamma},

where
A>0
is the entropy constant and
\gamma>1
is the adiabatic exponent.

\Psi
denotes the gravitational potential as

(1.3)

\Psi(t,\bm{x})=\displaystyle-G\int_{\Omega(t)}\frac{\rho(t,\bm{\hat{x}})}{|\bm{x}-\bm{\hat{x}}|}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\hat{x}}

with the gravitational constant
G>0
. Since

\rho>0
in
\Omega(t)
and

\rho\equiv 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Omega(t)
,
\Psi
satisfies

(1.4)

\Delta\Psi=4\pi G\rho\quad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$},\qquad\quad\lim_{|\bm{x}|\rightarrow\infty}\Psi(t,\bm{x})=0.

In particular,
\Psi(t,\bm{x})
is defined in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for each
t>0
.

\mathbb{T}
denotes the viscous stress tensor as:

(1.5)

\mathbb{T}=2\mu(\rho)D(\bm{u})+\lambda(\rho)\dive\bm{u}\,\mathbb{I}_{3},

where
D(\bm{u})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\bm{u}+(\nabla\bm{u})^{\top})
is the deformation tensor,
\mathbb{I}_{3}
is the
3\times 3
identity matrix,

(1.6)

\mu(\rho)=a_{1}\rho^{\delta},\qquad\lambda(\rho)=a_{2}\rho^{\delta},

for some constant
\delta>0
,
\mu(\rho)
is the shear viscosity coefficient,
\lambda(\rho)+\frac{2}{3}\mu(\rho)
is the bulk viscosity coefficient,
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are both constants satisfying

(1.7)

a_{1}>0,\qquad\quad 2a_{1}+3a_{2}\geq 0.

Equation
\eqref{eq:1.1-vfbp}_{3}
asserts that there is no vacuum inside the fluid,
\eqref{eq:1.1-vfbp}_{4}
is the vacuum boundary condition stating that
\rho
vanishes along the moving boundary
\partial\Omega(t)
,
\eqref{eq:1.1-vfbp}_{5}
is the kinetic boundary condition requiring that the moving boundary travels with the normal velocity of the fluid, and
\eqref{eq:1.1-vfbp}_{6}
provides the initial conditions for the density, velocity, and domain. We refer the reader to
[
3
,
10
]
for more details of the related background on
CNSP
.

With the local sound speed given by
c=\sqrt{P^{\prime}(\rho)}
and
c_{0}=c|_{t=0}
, satisfaction of the condition

(1.8)

-\infty<\frac{\partial c^{2}_{0}}{\partial\bm{n}}<0\qquad\text{on $\partial\Omega$}

defines a physical vacuum boundary (see
[
26
,
27
,
9
,
19
]
), which is a condition necessary for the gas particles on the boundary to accelerate.
In fact, this notion of physical vacuum can be realized by the stationary solutions of the compressible Euler–Poisson equations or
CNSP
for gaseous stars,
i.e.
, the Lane–Emden solution (see
[
3
,
24
,
29
]
).
The aim of this paper is to establish the local-in-time well-posedness of classical solutions with physical vacuum to
VFBP
(
1.1
) of the 3-D
CNSP
with degenerate viscosities in the spherically symmetric motion.

It is worth noting that, for barotropic flows, since
\rho_{0}>0
in
\Omega
, the physical vacuum boundary condition (
1.8
) implies that

\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}
tends to zero like the distance function
\text{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
near
\partial\Omega
, which leads to a strong degeneracy in the time evolution of hydrodynamic equations. Then it is intricate to provide an effective propagation mechanism for the regularity of
\bm{u}
near the vacuum,
which makes the study on the existence of classical solutions to the corresponding free boundary problem very difficult for both the inviscid and viscous flows.

For the
VFBP
of the isentropic compressible Euler equations ((
1.1
) with
a_{1}=a_{2}=G=0
), some significant advances on the well-posedness of smooth solutions satisfying (
1.8
) have been obtained. The local existence theory was developed by Coutand–Shkoller
[
8
,
9
]
and Jang–Masmoudi
[
18
,
19
]
, respectively. Recently, Jang–Hadžić
[
15
]
constructed unique global solutions when
\gamma\in(1,\frac{5}{3}]
, and the initial data lie sufficiently close to the expanding compactly supported affine motions constructed by Sideris
[
37
]
, and they satisfy (
1.8
). Later, by a different approach, Shkoller–Sideris
[
36
]
proved the stability of affine flows and global existence of classical solutions for all
\gamma>1
. On the other hand, for the
VFBP
of 3-D isentropic compressible Euler–Poisson equations, the local existence of classical solutions was established by Gu–Lei
[
13
]
, and the global existence of classical solutions was obtained by Hadžić–Jang
[
16
]
, which stay close to Sideris affine solutions of the Euler equations. Some other important developments can be found in
[
7
,
25
,
32
,
35
,
26
,
27
,
14
,
38
]
and the references therein.

For the corresponding
VFBP
of viscous compressible flows in a physical vacuum (as defined in (
1.8
)), under the assumption that the viscosity coefficients obey a power law of
\rho
(
i.e.
,
\rho^{\delta}
with some exponent
\delta>0
in (
1.6
)), a series of important progress on the well-posedness of strong or classical solutions has been achieved.
By taking the effect of gravity force into account, the global existence of the
1-D
strong solution of the barotropic compressible Navier–Stokes equations with small data was proved by Ou–Zeng
[
34
]
. Later, under a proper smallness assumption, Luo–Xin–Zeng
[
28
]
established the global existence of strong solutions satisfying (
1.8
) of the
3-D
spherically symmetric
CNSP
.
It is worth pointing out that the solutions obtained in
[
34
,
28
]
are just strong ones, which are not smooth all the way up to the moving boundary. To make sure that the solution is smooth all the way up to the moving boundary, by assuming that
\rho_{0}\in H^{5}(I)
and
u_{0}
belongs to a weighted
H^{6}(I)
space, Li–Wang–Xin
[
22
]
established the local well-posedness of classical solutions satisfying (
1.8
) to one-dimensional (
1-D
) viscous Saint–Venant system, and then extended this theory to the two-dimensional (2-D) problem in
[
23
]
under the assumption that
\rho_{0}\in H^{7}
and
u_{0}
belongs to a weighted
H^{8}
space.
Based on some new observations on the crossing derivatives estimates, when
\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}\in H^{3}
(
\beta\in(\frac{1}{3},1]
) and vanishes on the moving boundary as the distance function, Xin–Zhang–Zhu
[
41
]
established the global existence of classical solutions with large data to the
1-D
viscous Saint–Venant system. Recently, in Chen–Zhang–Zhu
[
4
]
, the global well-posedness of classical solutions in a physical vacuum to
VFBP
of the viscous Saint–Venant system with large data has been established in the spherically symmetric motion.
On the other hand, if the viscosity coefficients are all constants,
i.e.
,
\delta=0
in (
1.6
), Jang
[
17
]
established the local well-posedness of strong
solutions to
CNSP
in the spherically symmetric and barotropic motion.
Some other important developments can be found in
[
29
,
31
]
and the references therein.

It is worth noting that the corresponding problems will become considerably easier when
\rho_{0}
possesses a higher decay rate near the vacuum,
i.e.
,
\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}\sim(\mathrm{dist}(y,\Gamma))^{k}
with
k=2,3,\cdots
. For example, the initial sound-speed profile
c_{0}=\sqrt{A\gamma}\rho_{0}^{(\gamma-1)/2}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
k\geq 4
can be used to establish the local well-posedness of classical solutions for the Cauchy problem of the isentropic compressible Euler equations by the framework introduced by Makino–Ukai–Kawashima
[
30
]
, and also for the
CNS
by Geng–Li–Zhu
[
12
]
. However, if
\rho^{\gamma-1}
decays to zero like
(\mathrm{dist}(y,\Gamma))^{k}
with
k=2,3,\cdots
, the gas cannot accelerate into the vacuum region.

1.1.
Lagrangian Reformulation in Spherical Coordinates

In this paper, we will establish the local well-posedness of the spherically symmetric (classical) solutions of
VFBP
(
1.1
), taking the following form

(1.9)

(\rho,\bm{u})(t,\bm{x})=(\rho(t,|\bm{x}|),u(t,|\bm{x}|)\frac{\bm{x}}{|\bm{x}|}),

with the initial data:

(1.10)

(\rho,\bm{u})(0,\bm{x})=(\rho_{0},\bm{u}_{0})(\bm{x})=(\rho_{0}(|\bm{x}|),u_{0}(|\bm{x}|)\frac{\bm{x}}{|\bm{x}|}).

Our results hold for
(\delta,\gamma)
satisfying

(1.11)

\delta\in(0,1),\qquad\gamma\in(\frac{4}{3},\infty),

without any restriction on the size of the initial data. The initial density
\rho_{0}
we consider satisfies the following condition:

(1.12)

\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}(\bm{x})\in H^{3}(\Omega),\quad K_{1}(1-|\bm{x}|)\leq\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}\leq K_{2}(1-|\bm{x}|)\qquad\text{for all $\bm{x}\in\overline{\Omega}$},

for some constants
K_{2}>K_{1}>0
. It is worth noting that (
1.12
) implies that
\rho_{0}
satisfies the physical vacuum boundary condition for spherically symmetric flows,
i.e.
,

(1.13)

\rho^{\gamma-1}_{0}\sim 1-|\bm{x}|\qquad\text{as $|\bm{x}|$ near the vacuum boundary $|\bm{x}|=1$}.

Since we focus on the spherically symmetric flow, we first reformulate problem (
1.1
) into the following form in
I(t)=[0,R(t))
as the radial projection of the moving domain
\Omega(t)

with
R(0)=1
and
I=[0,1)
:

(1.14)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{t}+u\rho_{x}+\rho\big(u_{x}+\frac{2u}{x}\big)=0&\text{in }I(t),\\[1.0pt]
\displaystyle\rho u_{t}+\rho uu_{x}+P_{x}+\rho\Psi_{x}=(2a_{1}+a_{2})\Big(\rho^{\delta}\big(u_{x}+\frac{2u}{x}\big)\Big)_{x}-4a_{1}\frac{(\rho^{\delta})_{x}u}{x}&\text{in }I(t),\\[2.0pt]
\rho>0&\text{in }I(t),\\[2.0pt]
\rho=0&\text{on }\{x=R(t)\},\\[2.0pt]
R^{\prime}(t)=u(t,R(t))&\text{on }\{x=R(t)\},\\[2.0pt]
(\rho,u)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},u_{0})&\text{in }I(0):=I,\end{cases}

where
x=|\bm{x}|
and
\Psi_{x}
defined in the whole space is given by

(1.15)

\Psi_{x}(t,x)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{4\pi G}{x^{2}}\int_{0}^{x}\rho(t,\hat{x})\hat{x}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{x}\qquad&\text{for }x\in\bar{I}(t),\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{4\pi G}{x^{2}}\int_{0}^{R(t)}\rho(t,\hat{x})\hat{x}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{x}\qquad&\text{for }x\in[0,\infty)\backslash\bar{I}(t).\end{cases}

Moreover,
u|_{x=0}=0
, which can be derived from the continuity of
\bm{x}\mapsto\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})
at
\bm{x}=\bm{0}
.

Second, problem (
1.14
), formulated in Eulerian coordinates on the moving interval
I(t)
, can be transformed to a problem on the fixed interval
I
by introducing the Lagrangian coordinates. To this end, denote by
x=\eta(t,r)
the position of the fluid particle
x\in I(t)
at
t\geq 0
so that

(1.16)

\eta_{t}(t,r)=u(t,\eta(t,r)),\qquad\eta(0,r)=r,

and
(t,r)
are the Lagrangian coordinates. Moreover,
\eta(t,0)=0
for
t\in[0,T]
due to
u(t,0)=0
.
Then, by introducing the Lagrangian density, velocity, and gravitational potential,

(1.17)

\varrho(t,r)=\rho(t,\eta(t,r)),\qquad U(t,r)=u(t,\eta(t,r)),\qquad\Phi(t,r)=\Psi(t,\eta(t,r)),

problem (
1.14
) can be written in the following initial-boundary value problem (
IBVP
) in the fixed domain
I
in Lagrangian coordinates
(t,r)
:

(1.18)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\varrho_{t}+\varrho\big(\frac{U_{r}}{\eta_{r}}+\frac{2U}{\eta}\big)=0&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
\displaystyle\eta_{r}\varrho U_{t}+A(\varrho^{\gamma})_{r}+\varrho\Phi_{r}=(2a_{1}+a_{2})\Big(\varrho^{\delta}\big(\frac{U_{r}}{\eta_{r}}+\frac{2U}{\eta}\big)\Big)_{r}-4a_{1}\frac{(\varrho^{\delta})_{r}U}{\eta}&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
\eta_{t}=U&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
\varrho>0&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
\varrho|_{r=1}=0&\text{on }(0,T],\\[2.0pt]
(\varrho,U,\eta)(0,r)=(\rho_{0}(r),u_{0}(r),r)&\text{for $r\in I$},\end{cases}

where
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\gamma\in(\frac{4}{3},\infty)
, and
\Phi_{r}
is given by

(1.19)

\Phi_{r}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{4\pi G\eta_{r}}{\eta^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\varrho(t,\hat{r})\eta^{2}\eta_{r}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{r}&\text{for }r\in\bar{I},\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{4\pi G\eta_{r}}{\eta^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\varrho(t,\hat{r})\eta^{2}\eta_{r}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{r}&\text{for }r\in[0,\infty)\backslash\bar{I}.\end{cases}

It follows from the facts that
u|_{x=0}=0
and
\eta|_{r=0}=0
that
U(t,0)=u(t,\eta(t,0))=0
.

Moreover, it follows from Lemma
B.1
in Appendix
B
that condition (
1.12
), which is initially satisfied by
\rho_{0}(\bm{x})
in 3-D Eulerian coordinates, can be rewritten in spherical coordinates as a condition satisfied by
\rho_{0}(r)
: for some constants
K_{2}>K_{1}>0
,

(1.20)

\displaystyle r\big(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1},(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{r},(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{rr},\frac{(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{r}}{r},(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{rrr},(\frac{(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{r}}{r})_{r}\big)\in L^{2}(I),

\displaystyle K_{1}(1-r)\leq\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}(r)\leq K_{2}(1-r)\qquad\text{for all $r\in I$}.

The corresponding study of (
1.18
) is very difficult, since the structure of the momentum equation
\eqref{eq:VFBP-La}_{2}
is full of nonlinearity, degeneracy, and singularity. Thus, new ideas are required on the study of the well-posedness of smooth solutions to overcome these difficulties.

1.2.
Notations and Conventions

Before stating the main theorem, we first list the notations and conventions used throughout this paper.

1.2.1.
Notations on coordinates and operators

•

\bm{y}\in\Omega:=\{\bm{y}:\,|\bm{y}|<1\}
denotes the 3-D Lagrangian spatial coordinates (see Appendix B);

I:=[0,1)
; and
r=|\bm{y}|\in I
denotes the radial coordinate.

•

For any function
f(t,r)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\partial_{r}^{l}f=f_{t\underbrace{\text{\tiny$r\cdots r$}}_{\text{$l$-times}}},\quad D_{\eta}f:=\frac{f_{r}}{\eta_{r}},\quad D_{\eta}^{k}f:=D_{\eta}(D_{\eta}^{k-1}f)\quad\text{for $k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}$ and $k\geq 2$.}

Moreover, to simplify the notations, we define the operator
\mathscr{D}_{\eta}
as:

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\eta}f

\displaystyle:=(D_{\eta}f,\frac{f}{\eta}),\qquad\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f:=(D_{\eta}^{2}f,D_{\eta}(\frac{f}{\eta})),

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{3}f

\displaystyle:=(D_{\eta}^{3}f,\frac{D_{\eta}^{2}f}{\eta},D_{\eta}^{2}(\frac{f}{\eta}),\frac{1}{\eta}D_{\eta}(\frac{f}{\eta})),

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{4}f

\displaystyle:=(D_{\eta}^{4}f,D_{\eta}(\frac{D_{\eta}^{2}f}{\eta}),D_{\eta}^{3}(\frac{f}{\eta}),D_{\eta}(\frac{1}{\eta}D_{\eta}(\frac{f}{\eta}))).

\mathscr{D}_{r}^{k}
(
k=1,\cdots,4
) is defined in the same way as
\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{k}
(
k=1,\cdots,4
), except with
\eta(r)
in place of
r
.
Notice from Lemma
B.1
in Appendix
B
that the operator
\mathscr{D}_{\eta}
can be formally seen as a representative of the gradient
\nabla
in the radial Lagrangian coordinate. Besides, the following useful properties will be used in later analysis:

\displaystyle D_{\eta}(\mathscr{D}_{\eta}f)=\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f,\qquad D_{\eta}(\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{3}f)=\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{4}f,

\displaystyle|D_{\eta}f|^{2}+\Big|\frac{f}{\eta}\Big|^{2}=|\mathscr{D}_{\eta}f|^{2},\qquad|D_{\eta}^{2}f|^{2}+\Big|D_{\eta}(\frac{f}{\eta})\Big|^{2}=|\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f|^{2},

\displaystyle|D_{\eta}(\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f)|^{2}+\Big|\frac{\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f}{\eta}\Big|^{2}=|\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{3}f|^{2},\qquad|D_{\eta}^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f)|^{2}+\Big|D_{\eta}\big(\frac{\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f}{\eta}\big)\Big|^{2}=|\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{4}f|^{2}.

1.2.2.
Notations on function spaces

•

For any function space
X(I)
appearing in this paper, unless otherwise specified, the following notations are used:

\displaystyle X=X(I),\quad X^{*}\text{ --- the dual space of $X$},

\displaystyle X^{*}([0,T];Y^{*})\text{ --- the dual space of $X([0,T];Y)$},

\displaystyle|f|_{p}=\|f\|_{L^{p}},\quad\|f\|_{k,p}=\|f\|_{W^{k,p}},\quad\|f\|_{k}=\|f\|_{H^{k}},

\displaystyle L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}:=\big\{f:\,f\in L^{p}(K)\,\,\text{for any open interval $K$ such that $\bar{K}\subset I\backslash\{0\}$}\big\},

\displaystyle H^{k}_{\mathrm{loc}}:=\big\{f:\,\partial_{r}^{j}f\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\,\,\text{for any $0\leq j\leq k$}\big\},\qquad\|f\|_{X_{t}(Y)}=\|f\|_{X([0,T];Y(I))}.

•

Unless otherwise specified, the following definitions of weighted function spaces are used: let
J\subset I
and let
0\leq\mathrm{w}=\mathrm{w}(r)
be some function on
J
,

\displaystyle H^{k}_{\mathrm{w}}(J):=\big\{f:\,\sqrt{\mathrm{w}}\partial_{r}^{j}f\in L^{2}(J)\,\,\text{for $0\leq j\leq k$}\big\},\qquad H^{-k}_{\mathrm{w}}(J):=(H^{k}_{\mathrm{w}}(J))^{*},

\displaystyle L^{2}_{\mathrm{w}}(J)=H^{0}_{\mathrm{w}}(J),\quad\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathrm{w}}(J)}=\|\sqrt{\mathrm{w}}f\|_{L^{2}(J)},\quad\|f\|_{H^{k}_{\mathrm{w}}(J)}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\partial_{r}^{j}f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathrm{w}}(J)}.

In particular, if
J=I
, then

\displaystyle H^{k}_{\mathrm{w}}=H^{k}_{\mathrm{w}}(I),\quad H^{-k}_{\mathrm{w}}=H^{-k}_{\mathrm{w}}(I),\quad L^{2}_{\mathrm{w}}=L^{2}_{\mathrm{w}}(I),\quad|f|_{2,\mathrm{w}}=\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathrm{w}}},\quad\|f\|_{k,\mathrm{w}}=\|f\|_{H^{k}_{\mathrm{w}}}.

•

We denote by
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{w}}(J)
the space of all functions
f
satisfying
(f,f_{r},\frac{f}{r})\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{w}}(J)
for some interval
J=(0,a)
with
a\in(0,1)
:

\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{w}}(J):=\big\{f:\,\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{w}}(J)}<\infty\big\}\,\,\,\,\text{with}\,\,\,\,\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{w}}(J)}^{2}:=\int_{J}\mathrm{w}\Big(f^{2}+f_{r}^{2}+\frac{f^{2}}{r^{2}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r,

where
\mathrm{w}=\mathrm{w}(r)\geq 0
is a weight function on
J
, and we let
\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{\mathrm{w}}(J):=(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{w}}(J))^{*}
.

•

For any function space
X
and functions
(\varphi,g_{1},\cdots\!,g_{k})
,

\|\varphi(g_{1},\cdots\!,g_{k})\|_{X}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\varphi g_{i}\|_{X},\qquad|\varphi(g_{1},\cdots\!,g_{k})|:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\varphi g_{i}|.

•

Denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X^{*}\times X}
the duality pairing between the space
X
and its dual space
X^{*}
:

\displaystyle\left<F,f\right>_{X^{*}\times X}:=F(f)\ \ \text{for }F\in X^{*},\ \ f\in X.

If
F\in X^{*}
is identified with a
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-function (still denoted by
F
) in the canonical way and, for a given
f\in X
, the product
Ff\in L^{1}
, then the duality pairing reduces to

\left<F,f\right>_{X^{*}\times X}=\langle F,f\rangle:=\int_{0}^{1}Ff\,\mathrm{d}r.

\left<F,f\right>_{X_{t}^{*}(Y^{*})\times X_{t}(Y)}
denotes the pairing between
X([0,T];Y)
and
X^{*}([0,T];Y^{*})
.

1.2.3.
Other notations

•

B_{a}:=\{\bm{y}:\,|\bm{y}|<a\}
denotes the ball centered at the origin with radius
a
.

•

\delta_{ij}
denotes the Kronecker symbol with indices
(i,j)
:
\delta_{ij}=1
if
i=j
,
\delta_{ij}=0
if
i\neq j
.

•

For any
n\times n
real matrix
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{M}_{ij}
denotes its
(i,j)
-th entry. Moreover,
\mathrm{SO}(n)
denotes the set of all
n\times n
real orthogonal matrices
\mathcal{O}
such that
\det\mathcal{O}=1
, where
\det\mathcal{O}
is the determinant of
\mathcal{O}
.

•

\zeta_{a}=\zeta_{a}(r)\in C^{\infty}[0,1]
(
a\in(0,1)
) denotes a cut-off function satisfying

\zeta_{a}\in[0,1],\qquad(\zeta_{a})_{r}\leq 0,\qquad\zeta_{a}=1\ \ \text{on $[0,a]$},\qquad\zeta_{a}=0\ \ \text{on $\big[\frac{1+3a}{4},1\big]$},

and
\zeta_{a}^{\sharp}=\zeta_{a}^{\sharp}(r):=1-\zeta_{a}(r)
. In particular, if
a=\frac{1}{2}
, define

\zeta=\zeta(r):=\zeta_{\frac{1}{2}}(r),\qquad\zeta^{\sharp}=\zeta^{\sharp}(r):=1-\zeta(r).

•

\chi_{a}=\chi_{a}(r)
denotes the characteristic function on
[0,a]

(a\in(0,1))
,
i.e.
,
\chi_{a}=1
on
[0,a]
and
\chi_{a}=0
on
(a,1]
, and
\chi_{a}^{\sharp}=1-\chi_{a}
.
In particular, if
a=\frac{1}{2}
, define

\chi=\chi(r):=\chi_{\frac{1}{2}}(r),\qquad\chi^{\sharp}=\chi^{\sharp}(r):=1-\chi(r).

1.3.
Main Results

This section is devoted to stating our main results. First,
\eqref{eq:VFBP-La}_{1}
and
\eqref{eq:VFBP-La}_{3}
imply that

(1.21)

\varrho(t,r)=\frac{r^{2}\rho_{0}(r)}{\eta^{2}\eta_{r}}.

Then (
1.18
), along with (
1.21
), can be written as the following
IBVP
for
(U,\eta)
in
[0,T]\times I
:

(1.22)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\varrho U_{t}+AD_{\eta}(\varrho^{\gamma})+4\pi G\frac{\varrho}{\eta^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\hat{r}^{2}\rho_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{r}\\[1.0pt]
\displaystyle\quad=(2a_{1}+a_{2})D_{\eta}\Big(\varrho^{\delta}\big(D_{\eta}U+\frac{2U}{\eta}\big)\Big)-4a_{1}D_{\eta}(\varrho^{\delta})\frac{U}{\eta},\\[1.0pt]
\eta_{t}=U,\\[1.0pt]
(U,\eta)(0,r)=(u_{0}(r),r)\qquad\text{for $r\in I$}.\end{cases}

Second, we define classical solutions of problem (
1.22
) as follows:

Definition 1.1
.

Let
T>0
. A vector function
(U,\eta)(t,r)
is called
a classical solution of
IBVP
(
1.22
)
in
[0,T]\times\bar{I}
if the following properties hold
:

(i)

(U,\eta)(t,r)
satisfies equations

\eqref{eq:VFBP-La-eta}_{1}
–
\eqref{eq:VFBP-La-eta}_{2}
pointwise in
(0,T]\times\bar{I}
, and takes the initial data
\eqref{eq:VFBP-La-eta}_{3}
continuously
;

(ii)

\eta_{r}(t,r)
and
\frac{\eta}{r}(t,r)
are strictly positive in
[0,T]\times\bar{I}
:

\inf_{[0,T]\times\bar{I}}\ \eta_{r}>0,\qquad\inf_{[0,T]\times\bar{I}}\ \frac{\eta}{r}>0;

(iii)

(U,\eta)(t,r)
satisfies the following regularity properties
:

\displaystyle\big(U,U_{r},\frac{U}{r}\big)\in C([0,T];C(\bar{I})),\qquad\big(U_{rr},(\frac{U}{r})_{r},U_{t}\big)\in C((0,T];C(\bar{I})),

\displaystyle\big(\eta,\eta_{r},\frac{\eta}{r}\big)\in C^{1}([0,T];C(\bar{I})),\qquad\,\big(\eta_{rr},(\frac{\eta}{r})_{r}\big)\in C^{1}((0,T];C(\bar{I})).

Next, to clearly state our main results, we need to define the following nonlinear weighted energy functional and related parameters:

•

We fix a universal parameter
\varepsilon_{0}>0
throughout the paper satisfying

(1.23)

0<\varepsilon_{0}<\min\Big\{\frac{3-\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}{2},\,\frac{1-\delta}{\gamma-1},\,\frac{1}{100}\Big\}.

•

The total energy:

(1.24)

\mathcal{E}(t,f)=\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}(t,f)+\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ex}}(t,f),

where

(1.25)

\displaystyle\qquad\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}(t,f)

\displaystyle:=\|\zeta r(f,\mathscr{D}_{\eta}f,f_{t},\mathscr{D}_{\eta}f_{t},\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f,\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{3}f)(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2},

\displaystyle\qquad\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ex}}(t,f)

\displaystyle:=\big\|\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(f,f_{t})(t)\big\|_{L^{2}(\frac{1}{2},1)}^{2}+\big\|\rho_{0}^{\frac{\delta}{2}}(D_{\eta}f,D_{\eta}f_{t})(t)\big\|_{L^{2}(\frac{1}{2},1)}^{2}

\displaystyle+\big\|\rho_{0}^{(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0})(\gamma-1)}(D_{\eta}^{2}f,D_{\eta}^{3}f)(t)\big\|_{L^{2}(\frac{1}{2},1)}^{2},

and
\zeta=\zeta(r)\in C^{\infty}[0,1]
denotes a decreasing cut-off function satisfying

(1.26)

\zeta\in[0,1],\qquad\zeta(r)=1\ \ \text{for $r\in\big[0,\frac{1}{2}\big]$},\qquad\zeta(r)=0\ \ \text{for $r\in\big[\frac{5}{8},1\big]$}.

•

The total dissipation:

(1.27)

\mathcal{D}(t,f)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{in}}(t,f)+\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{ex}}(t,f),

where

(1.28)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{in}}(t,f)

\displaystyle:=\|\zeta r(f_{tt},\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{2}f_{t},\mathscr{D}_{\eta}^{4}f)(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2},

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{ex}}(t,f)

\displaystyle:=\big\|\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{tt}(t)\big\|_{L^{2}(\frac{1}{2},1)}^{2}+\big\|\rho_{0}^{(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0})(\gamma-1)}(D_{\eta}^{2}f_{t},D_{\eta}^{4}f)(t)\big\|_{L^{2}(\frac{1}{2},1)}^{2}.

Based on the choice of
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})(t,U)
in the above, we are now ready to state the main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
A>0
,
G>0
,
a_{1}>0
,
2a_{1}+3a_{2}>0
, and (
1.11
) hold.
If
\rho_{0}
satisfies (
1.20
) and
u_{0}(r)
satisfies

(1.29)

\mathcal{E}(0,U)<\infty,

then there exists
T_{*}>0
, which depends only on
(a_{1},a_{2},A,\delta,\gamma,\varepsilon_{0},\rho_{0},u_{0},K_{1},K_{2},G)
, such that
IBVP
(
1.22
) admits a unique classical solution
(U,\eta)(t,r)
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}
satisfying

(1.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t,U)+t\mathcal{D}(t,U)\in L^{\infty}(0,T_{*}),\qquad\mathcal{D}(t,U)\in L^{1}(0,T_{*}),

(1.31)

\displaystyle(\eta_{r},\frac{\eta}{r})(t,r)\in\big[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\big]\qquad\quad\ \ \,\text{for $(t,r)\in[0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}$}.

Moreover, such a classical solution admits the following boundary conditions
:

(1.32)

U|_{r=0}=\big(D_{\eta}U+\frac{2a_{2}}{2a_{1}+a_{2}}\frac{U}{\eta}\big)\Big|_{r=1}=0\qquad\text{on $(0,T_{*}]$}.

In fact, Theorem
1.1
can be extended to more general
\eta_{0}(r)
.

Theorem 1.2
.

If we assume that
\eta(0,r)=\eta_{0}(r)
with general initial map
\eta_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle((\eta_{0})_{r},\frac{\eta_{0}}{r})(r)\in[\delta_{*},\delta^{*}]\ \text{for $r\in\bar{I}$},\quad(\zeta r\mathscr{D}_{r}^{k}\eta_{0},\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta_{0},\rho_{0}^{\frac{\delta}{2}}(\eta_{0})_{r},\chi^{\sharp}\rho_{0}^{(\gamma-1)(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0})}\partial_{r}^{l}\eta_{0})\in L^{2},

for integers
k\in[0,4]
,
l\in[2,4]
,
and some constants
\delta^{*}>\delta_{*}>0
, then
Theorem
1.1
still holds. In this case, (
1.31
) is replaced by

(\eta_{r},\frac{\eta}{r})(t,r)\in\big[\frac{\delta_{*}}{2},\frac{3\delta^{*}}{2}\big]\qquad\quad\ \ \,\text{for $(t,r)\in[0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}$},

and
T_{*}>0
depends only on
(\delta_{*},\delta^{*},A,a_{1},a_{2},\delta,\gamma,\varepsilon_{0},\rho_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0},K_{1},K_{2},G)
.

This theorem can be proved by following the methodology developed in this paper with minor modifications.
Next, we make some remarks on Theorem
1.1
.

Remark 1.1
.

We show some explanations of the definitions of
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
given in (
1.24
)
–
(
1.28
).
Due to the coordinate singularity at the origin and the strong degeneracy on the moving boundary, we need to select the energy functionals separately in their respective neighborhoods and then combine them appropriately.

First, the interior energy functionals
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{in}})
are chosen based on the 3-D Lagrangian spatial coordinates
(t,\bm{y})
. If we let
\bm{U}(t,\bm{y}):=U(t,r)\frac{\bm{y}}{r}
, then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}(t,U)+t\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{in}}(t,U)\in L^{\infty}(0,T)
can be read as the following
H^{k}
-norms of
\bm{U}
due to
Lemma
B.1
in
Appendix
B
:

(1.33)

\sum_{l=0}^{1}\|\zeta\partial_{t}^{1-l}\bm{U}(t)\|_{H^{2l+1}(\Omega)}+t\sum_{l=0}^{2}\|\zeta\partial_{t}^{2-l}\bm{U}(t)\|_{H^{2l}(\Omega)}\in L^{\infty}(0,T).

Second, to ensure that the solution is classical in the region
\{r:\,\frac{1}{2}\leq r<1\}
and smooth up to the vacuum boundary at least when
t>0
, we establish some weighted
H^{4}(\frac{1}{2},1)
-estimates on
U
inspired by the following embedding, due to the Hardy and Sobolev inequalities
(
see
Lemmas
A.4
–
A.5
in
Appendix
A
):

H^{4}_{\rho_{0}^{2\alpha}}\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\hookrightarrow W^{3,1}\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\hookrightarrow C^{2}\big[\frac{1}{2},1\big]\quad\text{for some $\alpha<\frac{3}{2}(\gamma-1)$ and $\big|\alpha-\frac{3}{2}(\gamma-1)\big|\ll 1$},

where the weighted Sobolev space
H^{4}_{\rho_{0}^{2\alpha}}(\frac{1}{2},1)
is defined by

H^{4}_{\rho_{0}^{2\alpha}}\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big):=\big\{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big):\,\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{r}^{j}f\in L^{2}\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\ \text{for $0\leq j\leq 4$}\}.

Then the natural exterior energy and dissipation take the form
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{*}_{\mathrm{ex}}(t,U)

\displaystyle=\big|\chi^{\sharp}\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(U,U_{t})(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\big|\chi^{\sharp}\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(D_{\eta}U,D_{\eta}U_{t})(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\big|\chi^{\sharp}\rho_{0}^{\alpha}(D_{\eta}^{2}U,D_{\eta}^{3}U)(t)\big|_{2}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{*}_{\mathrm{ex}}(t,U)

\displaystyle=\big|\chi^{\sharp}\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}U_{tt}(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\big|\chi^{\sharp}\rho_{0}^{\alpha}(D_{\eta}^{2}U_{t},D_{\eta}^{4}U)(t)\big|_{2}^{2},

where
\alpha
can be determined in the elliptic estimates near the vacuum boundary. In fact, to derive the highest-order elliptic estimates, we reformulate
\eqref{eq:VFBP-La-eta}_{1}
by multiplying
\varrho^{\gamma-\delta-1}
:

(1.34)

\displaystyle(2a_{1}+a_{2})\varrho^{\gamma-1}D_{\eta}^{2}U

\displaystyle=\varrho^{\gamma-\delta}U_{t}-\frac{(2a_{1}+a_{2})\delta}{\gamma-1}D_{\eta}(\varrho^{\gamma-1})\big(D_{\eta}U+\frac{2a_{2}}{2a_{1}+a_{2}}\frac{U}{\eta}\big)

\displaystyle-2(2a_{1}+a_{2})\varrho^{\gamma-1}D_{\eta}\big(\frac{U}{\eta}\big)+\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}\varrho^{\gamma-\delta}D_{\eta}(\varrho^{\gamma-1})+\varrho^{\gamma-\delta}D_{\eta}\Phi.

Then we formally obtain from the above that

\displaystyle\varrho^{\alpha}D_{\eta}^{4}U

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2a_{1}+a_{2}}\varrho^{\alpha+1-\delta}D_{\eta}^{2}U_{t}+\mathrm{R}_{(\alpha)}=\underline{\frac{1}{(2a_{1}+a_{2})^{2}}\varrho^{\alpha+2-2\delta}U_{tt}}_{:=\clubsuit}+\widetilde{\mathrm{R}}_{(\alpha)},

where
(\mathrm{R}_{(\alpha)},\widetilde{\mathrm{R}}_{(\alpha)})
denote some harmless terms which possess either higher-order
\varrho
-weights or lower-order derivatives of
(\varrho,U)
. As can be checked,
\clubsuit
contains the highest-order tangential derivative and constitutes the main obstacle in controlling the
L^{2}(\frac{1}{2},1)
-norm of
\varrho^{\alpha}D_{\eta}^{4}U
.
Thus, (
1.20
) leads to

\displaystyle\clubsuit\in L^{2}\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)

\displaystyle\iff\alpha\geq\frac{1}{2}\implies\gamma-1>\frac{1}{3}.

Finally, by setting

\alpha:=(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0})(\gamma-1)<\frac{3}{2}(\gamma-1)

with some suitable small
\varepsilon_{0}
defined in (
1.23
), we recover the desired exterior energy and dissipation
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ex}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{ex}})(t,U)
in (
1.25
) and (
1.28
).

Remark 1.2
.

We give an example of the initial data required in
Theorem
1.1
. More precisely, we find that
Theorem
1.1
can be established if the initial data
belong to the following class
:

\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}(r)=1-r^{2},\qquad u_{0}(r)\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(0,1).

Clearly, we can check that
\rho_{0}
satisfies assumption (
1.20
). For
u_{0}
, it suffices to show that the initial values
(U_{t},U_{tr})(0,r)
satisfy (
1.29
). Indeed, we can obtain from
\eqref{eq:VFBP-La-eta}_{1}
and a direct calculation that
(U_{t},U_{tr})(0,r)
satisfy the following compatibility conditions
:

(1.35)

\displaystyle U_{t}(0,r)

\displaystyle=\frac{2a_{1}+a_{2}}{\rho_{0}}\Big(\rho_{0}^{\delta}\big((u_{0})_{r}+\frac{2a_{2}}{2a_{1}+a_{2}}\frac{u_{0}}{r}\big)\Big)_{r}+4a_{1}\rho_{0}^{\delta-1}(\frac{u_{0}}{r})_{r}

\displaystyle-\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{r}-\frac{4\pi G}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\hat{r}^{2}\rho_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{r},

\displaystyle U_{tr}(0,r)

\displaystyle=(2a_{1}+a_{2})\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\big(\rho_{0}^{\delta}(u_{0})_{r}\big)_{r}+\frac{2a_{2}}{2a_{1}+a_{2}}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\big(\rho_{0}^{\delta}\frac{u_{0}}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)_{r}+4a_{1}\Big(\rho_{0}^{\delta-1}(\frac{u_{0}}{r})_{r}\Big)_{r}

\displaystyle-\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}(\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1})_{rr}-4\pi G\rho_{0}+\frac{8\pi G}{r^{3}}\int_{0}^{r}\hat{r}^{2}\rho_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{r}.

It is then straightforward to verify that

\zeta rU_{t}(0,r),\ \zeta r\mathscr{D}_{r}U_{t}(0,r)\in L^{2}(I),\qquad\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}U_{t}(0,r),\ \rho_{0}^{\frac{\delta}{2}}U_{tr}(0,r)\in L^{2}(\frac{1}{2},1),

due to the regularities of
\rho_{0}
and the fact that
u_{0}
is compactly supported in
(0,1)
.

Remark 1.3
.

We briefly explain how to derive the boundary condition in (
1.32
). First, it follows from (
1.16
), (
1.20
),
\eqref{b1-lo}_{2}
, and
Definition
1.1
that

(1.36)

(\varrho^{\gamma-1},\,D_{\eta}\varrho^{\gamma-1},\,U,\ D_{\eta}U,\,D_{\eta}^{2}U,\,U_{t})\in C((0,T]\times[\frac{1}{2},1]).

Next, we reformulate
\eqref{eq:VFBP-La-eta}_{1}
by multiplying
\varrho^{\gamma-\delta-1}
:

(1.37)

\displaystyle\varrho^{\gamma-\delta}U_{t}+\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}\varrho^{\gamma-\delta}D_{\eta}(\varrho^{\gamma-1})+\varrho^{\gamma-\delta}D_{\eta}\Phi

\displaystyle=(2a_{1}+a_{2})\varrho^{\gamma-1}\big(D_{\eta}^{2}U+2D_{\eta}(\frac{U}{\eta})\big)+\frac{\delta}{\gamma-1}D_{\eta}(\varrho^{\gamma-1})\big((2a_{1}+a_{2})D_{\eta}U+2a_{2}\frac{U}{\eta}\big).

Then, taking the limit
r\to 1
in (
1.37
) and using (
1.20
), (
1.36
), and the lower bounds of
(\eta,\eta_{r})
near the boundary, we obtain

D_{\eta}(\varrho^{\gamma-1})\big(D_{\eta}U+\frac{2a_{2}}{2a_{1}+a_{2}}\frac{U}{\eta}\big)\big|_{r=1}=0.

Since
\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}\sim 1-r
and, by (
1.21
),
D_{\eta}(\varrho^{\gamma-1})|_{r=1}\neq 0
, the boundary condition
follows directly.

Remark 1.4
.

For
VFBP
(
1.1
), the usual
stress-free boundary condition holds automatically
:

(\mathbb{T}-P\mathbb{I}_{3})\cdot\bm{n}=\big(2a_{1}\rho^{\delta}D(\bm{u})+a_{2}\rho^{\delta}\dive\bm{u}\,\mathbb{I}_{3}-A\rho^{\gamma}\mathbb{I}_{3}\big)\cdot\bm{n}=\bm{0}.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows:
§
2
is devoted to constructing global approximation solutions for the corresponding linearized problem. In §
3
, we establish the uniform energy estimates for the solutions of the linearized problem introduced in §
2
.
Finally, in §
4
, based on the uniform estimates obtained above, we will give the proof for the local-in-time well-posedness of the classical solutions to the nonlinear problem (
1.22
) by the classical Picard iteration.
Finally, several auxiliary lemmas and useful coordinate transformations for spherically symmetric functions are collected in Appendices
A
–
D
.

2.
Linearization and Global Approximation Solutions

This section is devoted to constructing global-in-time approximation solutions for the corresponding linearized problem. In §
2
,
C\in(1,\infty)
denotes a generic constant depending only on
(a_{1},a_{2},A,\delta,\gamma,\rho_{0},u_{0},K_{1},K_{2},G)
, and
C(l_{1},\cdots\!,l_{k})\in(1,\infty)
a generic constant depending on
C

and additional parameters
(l_{1},\cdots\!,l_{k})
, which may be different at each occurrence.

2.1.
Linearization

In order to solve the nonlinear problem (
1.22
), we first need to study the following linearized problem in
[0,T]\times I
:

(2.1)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle r^{2}\rho_{0}U_{t}+A\big(\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\varrho}^{\gamma}\big)_{r}-2A\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\gamma}+4\pi G\frac{r^{2}\rho_{0}}{\bar{\eta}^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\hat{r}^{2}\rho_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{r}\\[10.0pt]
\displaystyle\quad=\Big(\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\big((2a_{1}+a_{2})D_{\bar{\eta}}U+2a_{2}\frac{U}{\bar{\eta}}\big)\Big)_{r}-\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(2a_{2}D_{\bar{\eta}}U+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\frac{U}{\bar{\eta}}\Big),\\[8.0pt]
U|_{t=0}=u_{0}\qquad\text{in }I,\end{cases}

where

(2.2)

\bar{\varrho}:=\frac{r^{2}\rho_{0}}{\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}},

\bar{\eta}
stands for the flow map corresponding to
\bar{U}
:

(2.3)

\bar{\eta}(t,r)=r+\int_{0}^{t}\bar{U}(s,r)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad\bar{\eta}(0,r)=r,\quad\bar{\eta}(t,0)=0,

and
\bar{U}
is a given function satisfying
\bar{U}(0,r)=u_{0}(r)
for
r\in I

and, for any
T>0
,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(t,\bar{U})+t\bar{\mathcal{D}}(t,\bar{U})\in L^{\infty}(0,T),\qquad\bar{\mathcal{D}}(t,\bar{U})\in L^{1}(0,T),

\displaystyle(\bar{U},\mathscr{D}_{r}\bar{U})\in C([0,T];C(\bar{I})),\qquad\ \,(\mathscr{D}_{r}^{2}\bar{U},\bar{U}_{t})\in C((0,T];C(\bar{I})),\quad\bar{U}|_{r=0}=0,

where
(\bar{\mathcal{E}},\bar{\mathcal{D}})(t,f)
are defined in the same way as
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})(t,f)
in (
1.24
) and (
1.27
), except with
\eta
in place of
\bar{\eta}
. Besides,
we define
(\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{in}},\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ex}},\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathrm{in}},\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathrm{ex}})(t,f)
in the similar manner to
(\bar{\mathcal{E}},\bar{\mathcal{D}})(t,f)
.

Clearly, (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) also provide the regularity of
\bar{\eta}
, that is,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\zeta r(\bar{\eta},\mathscr{D}_{r}\bar{\eta},\mathscr{D}_{r}^{2}\bar{\eta},\mathscr{D}_{r}^{3}\bar{\eta},\mathscr{D}_{r}^{4}\bar{\eta})\in C([0,T];L^{2}),

\displaystyle\chi^{\sharp}(\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\eta},\rho_{0}^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\bar{\eta}_{r})\in C([0,T];L^{2}),\qquad\chi^{\sharp}\rho_{0}^{(\gamma-1)(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0})}(\bar{\eta}_{rr},\bar{\eta}_{rrr},\bar{\eta}_{rrrr})\in C([0,T];L^{2}),

\displaystyle(\bar{\eta},\mathscr{D}_{r}\bar{\eta})\in C^{1}([0,T];C(\bar{I})),\qquad\mathscr{D}_{r}^{2}\bar{\eta}\in C^{1}((0,T];C(\bar{I})).

Moreover, we assume here that

(2.6)

(\bar{\eta}_{r},\frac{\bar{\eta}}{r})(t,r)\in\big[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\big]\qquad\text{for all }(t,r)\in[0,T]\times\bar{I}.

This requirement will be fulfilled in §
3
for the corresponding linearization procedure.

Now we define the classical solution of the linearized problem (
2.1
), which slightly differs from Definition
1.1
.

Definition 2.1
.

We say that
U(t,r)
is a classical solution of the linearized problem (
2.1
) in
[0,T]\times\bar{I}
if
U(t,r)
satisfies equation
\eqref{lp}_{1}
pointwise in
(0,T]\times\bar{I}
, and takes the initial data
\eqref{lp}_{2}
continuously, and

(2.7)

(U,\mathscr{D}_{r}U)\in C([0,T];C(\bar{I})),\qquad\mathscr{D}_{r}^{2}U\in C((0,T];C(\bar{I})).

Then the main conclusion in §
2
can be stated in the following lemma:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
A>0
,
G>0
,
a_{1}>0
,
2a_{1}+3a_{2}>0
, and (
1.11
) hold. Assume that
(\rho_{0},u_{0})(r)
satisfy (
1.20
) and
\mathcal{E}(0,U)<\infty
. Then, for any
T>0
, problem (
2.1
) admits a unique classical solution
U
in
[0,T]\times\bar{I}
satisfying

(2.8)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(t,U)+t\,\bar{\mathcal{D}}(t,U)\in L^{\infty}(0,T),\qquad\bar{\mathcal{D}}(t,U)\in L^{1}(0,T),

\displaystyle U|_{r=0}=\big(D_{\bar{\eta}}U+\frac{2a_{2}}{2a_{1}+a_{2}}\frac{U}{\bar{\eta}}\big)\Big|_{r=1}=0\qquad\qquad\text{for $t\in(0,T]$}.

2.2.
The Modified Galerkin Scheme for Some General Problems

To establish the well-posedness of (
2.1
) schematically, we initiate to study a general
IBVP
in
[0,T]\times I
:

(2.9)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}-2(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{g}{\bar{\eta}}+\Big((r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{h}{\bar{\eta}_{r}}\Big)_{r}\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle=\Big(\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\big((2a_{1}+a_{2})D_{\bar{\eta}}w+2a_{2}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}}\big)\Big)_{r}-\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(2a_{2}D_{\bar{\eta}}w+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}}\Big),\\[8.0pt]
w|_{t=0}=w_{0}\qquad\text{on }I,\end{cases}

where
(g,h,w_{0})
are given functions satisfying

(g,h)\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}),\qquad w_{0}\in L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}}.

First, we study the weak solutions of problem (
2.9
).

Definition 2.2
.

We say that a function
w(t,r)
is a weak solution in
[0,T]\times I
of
problem (
2.9
) if the following three properties hold
:

(i)

w\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})\cap L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
and
r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}});

(ii)

for all
\varphi
satisfying
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
and a.e. time
t\in(0,T)
,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t},\varphi\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}\times\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\big>+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\Big>+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle=\Big<g,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>+\big<h,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\big>;

(iii)

w(0,r)=w_{0}(r)
for a.e.
r\in I
.

Now we establish the following existence of weak solutions and their related estimates.

Proposition 2.1
.

For all
T>0
, problem (
2.9
) admits a unique weak solution
w
in
[0,T]\times I
, satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|w|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\big(\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}+\big\|r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\Big(|w_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}|(g,h)|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big).

Proof.

We divide the proof into four steps.

Step 1. Introduction of the Galerkin scheme.
First, it follows from Lemma
A.1
that, for given
w_{0}\in L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}}
, there exists a smooth sequence
\{w_{0}^{\vartheta}\}_{\vartheta>0}\subset C^{\infty}(\bar{I})
satisfying

(2.11)

\lim_{\vartheta\to 0}|w^{\vartheta}_{0}-w_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}=0.

Next, we construct a basis required in the Galerkin scheme. To match the spherical symmetry structure of
\eqref{galerkin-w}_{1}
, we consider the following Sturm–Liouville problem:

(2.12)

-(r^{2}\xi_{r})_{r}+2\xi=\nu\,r^{2}\xi\quad\text{on $I$},\qquad\ \ \xi|_{r=0}=\xi_{r}|_{r=1}=0.

Based on the Sturm–Liouville theorem, we can construct a Hilbert basis
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
of
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
, which is orthonormal in
L^{2}_{r^{2}}
and orthogonal in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
:

Lemma 2.2

(
[
42
]
)
.

Consider the Sturm–Liouville problem (
2.12
). Then all eigenvalues
\nu
of problem (
2.12
) are nonzero, real, and have multiplicity one.
Moreover, there are infinite but countable eigenvalues

\{\nu_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
, which are bounded below, strictly increasing and
\nu_{j}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
. Besides, the following approximation results hold
:

(i)

There exists a sequence of eigenfunctions
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
, corresponding to the eigenvalues
\{\nu_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
. Such a sequence of eigenfunctions
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is orthonormal and complete in
L^{2}_{r^{2}}
, namely,
\langle r^{2}\xi_{j},\xi_{k}\rangle=\delta_{kj}
for
k,j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and

\lim_{N\to\infty}|f_{N}-f|_{2,r^{2}}=0\qquad\text{for any $f\in L^{2}_{r^{2}}$},\qquad f_{N}:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\langle r^{2}f,\xi_{j}\rangle\xi_{j}.

(ii)

Such a sequence of eigenfunctions
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is also orthogonal and complete in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
, namely,
\langle r^{2}(\xi_{j})_{r},(\xi_{k})_{r}\rangle+2\big<\xi_{j},\xi_{k}\big>=\nu_{j}\delta_{jk}
for
k,j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and

\lim_{N\to\infty}\|f_{N}-f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}}=0\qquad\text{for any $f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}$}.

Proof.

We note that the Sturm–Liouville problem (
2.12
) is equivalent to the classical eigenvalue problem of the first-order spherical Bessel equation:

(2.13)

(r^{2}\xi_{r})_{r}+(\nu\,r^{2}-2)\xi=0\quad\text{on $I$},\qquad\ \ \xi|_{r=0}=\xi_{r}|_{r=1}=0.

Then the existence of the desired eigenvalues
\{\nu_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
and the eigenfunctions
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
satisfying the property (i) follows from the classical theories on the Sturm–Liouville problem or the Bessel equation, which can be found in
[
42
,
40
]
.

Thus, we only need to prove the desired property in (ii). The orthogonality

\langle r^{2}(\xi_{j})_{r},(\xi_{k})_{r}\rangle+2\big<\xi_{j},\xi_{k}\big>=\nu_{j}\delta_{jk}

follows from the facts that
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
solves the eigenvalue problem (
2.12
) and is orthonormal in
L^{2}_{r^{2}}
. To show that
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is complete in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
, we define the bilinear form
B[\cdot,\cdot]
by

B[f,g]:=\langle r^{2}f_{r},g_{r}\rangle+2\langle f,g\rangle.

Then, it follows from Lemma
A.5
and the Hölder inequality that

\displaystyle B[f,g]\leq|f_{r}|_{2,r^{2}}|g_{r}|_{2,r^{2}}+2|f|_{2}|g|_{2}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}},

\displaystyle B[f,f]=|f_{r}|_{2,r^{2}}^{2}+2|f|_{2}^{2}\geq C^{-1}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}}^{2},

which implies that
B[f,g]
is bounded and coercive in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
.

Finally, suppose

(2.14)

B[\xi_{j},f]=0\qquad\text{for all $j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}$}.

Since
\xi_{j}
is the eigenfunction that corresponds to the eigenvalue
\nu_{j}
, we have

B[\xi_{j},f]=\nu_{j}\langle r^{2}\xi_{j},f\rangle\qquad\ \text{for all $f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}$},

which, along with (
2.14
), yields

\langle r^{2}\xi_{j},f\rangle=0\qquad\text{for all $j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}$},

Thanks to the completeness of
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
in
L^{2}_{r^{2}}
, we obtain from the above that
f=0
. Hence, sequence
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is complete in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
.
∎

Consequently, based on the above basis
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
, define

(2.15)

w^{N,\vartheta}(t,r):=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mu_{k}^{N,\vartheta}(t)\xi_{k}(r)\qquad\text{for }\vartheta\in(0,1)\text{ and }N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

Here,
\mu_{k}^{N,\vartheta}(t)
are chosen by solving the following ODE problem in
[0,T]
:

(2.16)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}^{N,\vartheta},\xi_{j}\big>+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta},D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}\big>+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\\
\displaystyle\quad+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}\Big>+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta},\frac{\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\\
\displaystyle=\Big<g,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>+\big<h,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}\big>,\\[8.0pt]
\mu_{j}^{N,\vartheta}(0)=\langle r^{2}w_{0}^{\vartheta},\xi_{j}\rangle,\ \ j=1,2,\cdots\!,N.\end{cases}

The above system can also be rewritten as

(2.17)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mu^{N,\vartheta}(t)+\mathfrak{B}(t)\cdot\mu^{N,\vartheta}(t)=\mathfrak{c}(t)\qquad\text{in $(0,T]$},\\[10.0pt]
\mu_{j}^{N,\vartheta}(0)=\langle r^{2}w_{0}^{\vartheta},\xi_{j}\rangle\ \qquad\text{for $j=1,2,\cdots\!,N$},\end{cases}

where
\mu^{N,\vartheta}(t):=(\mu_{1}^{N,\vartheta},\cdots\!,\mu_{N}^{N,\vartheta})^{\top}(t)
,

\mathfrak{A}=(\mathfrak{A}_{kj})_{1\leq k,j\leq N},\qquad\mathfrak{B}(t)=(\mathfrak{B}_{kj}(t))_{1\leq k,j\leq N},\qquad\mathfrak{c}(t)=(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\cdots\!,\mathfrak{c}_{N})^{\top}(t),

and

(2.18)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{kj}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}r^{2}\rho_{0}\xi_{k}\xi_{j}\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mathfrak{c}_{j}(t):=\int_{0}^{1}(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(g\frac{2\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}+hD_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{kj}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big((2a_{1}+a_{2})D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{k}D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\frac{\xi_{k}\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}^{2}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(\frac{\xi_{k}}{\bar{\eta}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}+D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{k}\frac{\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r.

To solve (
2.17
), thanks to the facts that
\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}\sim 1-r
and the linear independency of
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
, we see that
\{(r^{2}\rho_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
are linearly independent, and hence its Gram matrix
\mathfrak{A}
is non-singular. Next, it follows from (
2.3
) and Lemma
2.2
that
\mathfrak{B}(t)\in C^{1}[0,T]
and
\mathfrak{c}(t)\in L^{2}(0,T)
. Therefore, by the classical ODE theory, the following existence result for (
2.17
) holds:

Lemma 2.3

(
[
5
]
)
.

Problem (
2.17
) admits a unique solution
\mu_{j}^{N,\vartheta}\in AC[0,T]
, for each
j=1,2,\cdots\!,N
,
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and
\vartheta\in(0,1)
, where
AC
denotes the space of absolutely continuous functions. As a consequence,
w^{N,\vartheta}\in AC([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}})
and
w^{N,\vartheta}
is differentiable a.e. in
t
, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\vartheta\in(0,1)
.

Step 2. Uniform estimates of
w^{N,\vartheta}
.
First, multiplying (
2.16
) by
\mu^{N,\vartheta}_{j}(t)
and summing the resulting equality with respect to
j
from
1
to
N
imply

(2.19)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{0}^{1}r^{2}\rho_{0}|w^{N,\vartheta}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r+L_{1}=L_{2},

where

(2.20)

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\big((2a_{1}+a_{2})X^{2}+4a_{2}XY+4(a_{1}+a_{2})Y^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}r,

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(2gY+hX\big)\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad X:=D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta},\quad Y:=\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}}.

For
L_{1}
, thanks to (
2.6
),
a_{1}>0
, and
2a_{1}+3a_{2}>0
, we can find a constant
c_{*}>0
, depending only on
(a_{1},a_{2})
, such that

(2.21)

L_{1}\geq c_{*}|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}^{2}.

For
L_{2}
, it follows from the Young inequality that

(2.22)

L_{2}\leq C|(g,h)|_{2}^{2}+\frac{c_{*}}{10}|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}^{2}.

Besides, by Lemmas
A.4
–
A.5
, we have

(2.23)

\displaystyle|w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(|\chi w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}}+|\chi^{\sharp}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,\rho_{0}^{\delta}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\big|\chi\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{r}\big|_{2,r^{2}}+|\chi^{\sharp}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,\rho_{0}}+|\chi^{\sharp}w_{r}^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,\rho_{0}^{\delta}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(|w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}+|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}\big).

Collecting (
2.19
) and (
2.21
)–(
2.22
) and integrating over
[0,t]
, combined with (
2.23
), imply

(2.24)

|w^{N,\vartheta}(t)|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|w^{N,\vartheta}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}\!\mathrm{d}t\leq C(T)\Big(|w^{N,\vartheta}(0)|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}|(g,h)|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big),

where

w^{N,\vartheta}(0,r)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu_{j}^{N,\vartheta}(0)e_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\langle w_{0}^{\vartheta},\xi_{j}\rangle\xi_{j}
.

Next, to derive the
L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}}
-boundedness of
w^{N,\vartheta}(0,r)
, we first see from (
2.11
) that, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
satisfying

(2.25)

|w^{\vartheta}_{0}-w_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\qquad\text{ for any $0<\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}$}.

Then, for such
\varepsilon,\vartheta_{0}>0
and fixed
\vartheta\in(0,\vartheta_{0}]
, by
w_{0}^{\vartheta}\in L^{2}\subset L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}}
and Lemma
2.2
, there exists a large
N_{0}=N_{0}(\varepsilon,\vartheta_{0})\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
so that

|w^{N,\vartheta}(0)-w^{\vartheta}_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}=\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{N}\langle w_{0}^{\vartheta},\xi_{j}\rangle\xi_{j}-w_{0}^{\vartheta}\Big|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\qquad\text{for any $N\geq N_{0}$},

which, combined with (
2.25
), yields

|w^{N,\vartheta}(0)-w_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}\leq|w^{N,\vartheta}(0)-w^{\vartheta}_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}+|w^{\vartheta}_{0}-w_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}<\varepsilon.

Hence, setting
\varepsilon:=\min\{|w_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}},\frac{1}{2}\}
, we obtain that there exist
\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(\varepsilon,\vartheta_{0})\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that

(2.26)

|w^{N,\vartheta}(0)|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}\leq 2|w_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}\quad\text{for any $\vartheta\in(0,\vartheta_{0}]$ and $N\geq N_{0}$}.

Finally, substituting (
2.26
) into (
2.24
) yields

(2.27)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|w^{N,\vartheta}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(T)\Big(|w_{0}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}|(g,h)|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big).

Step 3. Taking the limit as
N,\vartheta^{-1}\to\infty
.

Since
(L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}},L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
are separable reflexive Banach spaces due to Lemma
A.1
, it follows from (
2.27
) and the weak convergence arguments that there exists a subsequence (still denoted by)
w^{N,\vartheta}
and some limits
w
and
(X_{1},X_{2})
such that

(2.28)

\displaystyle w^{N,\vartheta}\rightharpoonup w

\displaystyle\text{weakly* in }L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}}),

\displaystyle\big(w^{N,\vartheta},w^{N,\vartheta}_{r},\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{r}\big)\rightharpoonup(w,X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle\text{weakly \, in }L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}).

Then the definition of weak derivatives implies that
(X_{1},X_{2})=(w_{r},\frac{w}{r})
.
Clearly,
w
also satisfies (
2.27
) due to the lower semi-continuity of weak convergence (
2.28
).

Now we pass to the limit
N,\vartheta^{-1}\to\infty
in (
2.16
). Let
\varphi^{M}(t,r)=\sum_{j=1}^{M}\varphi_{j}(t)\xi_{j}(r)
with
\varphi_{j}\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(0,T)
for
j=1,\cdots\!,M
and
M\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
Then by (
2.16
), for any
N\geq M
,

(2.29)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w^{N,\vartheta}_{t},\varphi^{M}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta},D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta},\frac{\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\Big<g,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\big<h,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t.

Since
(w^{N,\vartheta},\varphi^{M})
are differentiable with respect to
t
, we can transfer
\partial_{t}
in (
2.29
) from
w^{N,\vartheta}
to
\varphi^{M}
via integration by parts. Then, letting
N,\vartheta^{-1}\to\infty
, we see from (
2.28
) that

(2.30)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\langle r^{2}\rho_{0}w,\varphi^{M}_{t}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,\frac{\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\Big<g,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\big<h,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t.

Next, since
r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}\in H^{-1}([0,T];L^{2})
due to
w\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})
, by (
2.6
), (
2.30
), and the definition of distributional derivatives, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big|\langle r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t},\varphi^{M}\rangle_{H_{t}^{-1}((H^{1})^{*})\times H^{1}_{0,t}(H^{1})}\Big|=\Big|\int_{0}^{T}\langle r^{2}\rho_{0}w,\varphi^{M}_{t}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|w\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})}+\|(g,h)\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}\big)\|\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})}.

We now claim that

(2.31)

\left\{\varphi^{M}\right\}_{M\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\text{ is dense in }L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}).

Indeed, it suffices to prove the density of
\mathrm{span}\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
. Thanks to Lemma
A.2
, for any given
f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
, there exists a sequence
\{f^{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}\subset C^{\infty}(\bar{I})\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
such that

f^{\varepsilon}\to f\ \ \text{in $\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}$}\qquad\text{as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

Next, for any
f\in C^{\infty}(\bar{I})\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}
, we obtain from Lemma
2.2
and
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
that

\sum_{j=1}^{M}\langle f,\xi_{j}\rangle\xi_{j}\to f\,\,\,\,\text{in $\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}$}\qquad\,\,\text{as $M\to\infty$}.

Consequently, if setting
f^{M,\varepsilon}:=\sum_{j=1}^{M}\langle f^{\varepsilon},\xi_{j}\rangle\xi_{j}
, then
\{f^{M,\varepsilon}\}\subset\mathrm{span}\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
will be a sequence that converges to
f
in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
as
M,\varepsilon^{-1}\to\infty
, which shows the claim.

Thus, by Hahn–Banach theorem (see
[
6
]
), we see that
r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}
, a functional initially defined on
H^{1}_{0}([0,T];L^{2})
, can be uniquely extended to a functional defined on
L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
and

(2.32)

\|r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})}\leq C\big(\|w\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})}+\|(g,h)\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}\big)\leq C.

Finally, for any
\hat{\varphi}\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
,
there exists a sequence
\{\varphi^{M}\}_{M\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}

with each
\varphi^{M}\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
such that
\varphi^{M}\to\hat{\varphi}
in
L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
as
M\to\infty
. Passing to the limit
M\to\infty
in (
2.30
), along with (
2.32
), implies that, for all
\hat{\varphi}\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
,

(2.33)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t},\hat{\varphi}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}\times\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,D_{\bar{\eta}}\hat{\varphi}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\hat{\varphi}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},D_{\bar{\eta}}\hat{\varphi}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,\frac{\hat{\varphi}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\Big<g,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\hat{\varphi}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle h,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\hat{\varphi}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, the weak formulation (
2.10
) follows simply by setting
\hat{\varphi}(t,r)=\varphi(r)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
in (
2.33
) and applying
\partial_{t}
to both sides of the resulting equality.

Step 4. Uniqueness and time continuity.

First, it follows from Lemma
A.10
(let
s=1
in Lemma
A.10
),
w\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
, and
r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
that
w\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})
.

Next, we show that
w(0,r)=w_{0}(r)
for
a.e.

r\in I
. To this end, thanks to (
2.10
) and
w\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})
, for any
\hat{\varphi}\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{1}([0,T);\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
,

(2.34)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w,\hat{\varphi}_{t}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,D_{\bar{\eta}}\hat{\varphi}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\hat{\varphi}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},D_{\bar{\eta}}\hat{\varphi}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,\frac{\hat{\varphi}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\underline{\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w(0),\hat{\varphi}(0)\big>}_{:=\mathring{L}}+\int_{0}^{T}\Big<g,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\hat{\varphi}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle h,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\hat{\varphi}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

On the other hand, let
\varphi^{M}(t,x)=\sum_{j=1}^{M}\varphi_{j}(t)\xi_{j}
in (
2.29
) with
\varphi_{j}(t)\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}([0,T))
(
j=1,\cdots\!,M
) satisfy
\varphi^{M}\to\hat{\varphi}
in
C^{1}_{\mathrm{c}}([0,T);\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
as
M\to\infty
. Then by (
2.29
), for all
M\leq N
,

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w^{N,\vartheta},\varphi^{M}_{t}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta},D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta},\frac{\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w^{N,\vartheta}(0),\varphi^{M}(0)\big>+\int_{0}^{T}\Big<g,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\varphi^{M}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\big<h,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi^{M}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t.

Passing to the limits
M,N\to\infty
in the above implies that, for all
\hat{\varphi}\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{c}}([0,T);\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
, (
2.34
) still holds except for replacing term
\mathring{L}
with
\langle r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{0},\hat{\varphi}(0)\rangle
. Hence, this yields that
w(0,r)=w_{0}
for
a.e.

r\in I
. Finally, setting
w_{0}=0
,
\varphi=w
, and
g=h=0
in (
2.10
) implies
w=0
, which leads to the uniqueness.
∎

Next, if
w_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
, we can improve the regularity of weak solutions in Proposition
2.1
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
w
be the weak solution of (
2.16
) obtained in
Proposition
2.1
. Assume that
w_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
,
(g,h)\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
, and
(g_{t},h_{t})\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2})
. Then

(2.35)

\displaystyle w\in L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}),\quad w_{t}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}}),

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}w_{t}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})\cap L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}),\quad\sqrt{t}(r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{tt})\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}).

Moreover,
w
satisfies the following weak formulation for any a.e.
t>0
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\ \big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{tt},\varphi\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}\times\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}+\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta},(2a_{1}+a_{2})D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\frac{w_{t}}{\bar{\eta}}\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\\
&\quad+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta},\frac{w_{t}}{\bar{\eta}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi+D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\end{aligned}

(2.36)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\quad+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-(\delta+1)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(2-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)D_{\bar{\eta}}w,D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\Big>\\
&\quad+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big((1-\delta)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}-2\delta\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\quad+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-\delta D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(1-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big),\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi+D_{\bar{\eta}}w\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\\
&=\Big<(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(g_{t}-g\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\big),\frac{2\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>+\big<(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(h_{t}-hD_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}\big),D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\big>,\end{aligned}

and satisfies the following energy equality for a.e.
t>0
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{0}^{1}r^{2}\rho_{0}|w_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big((2a_{1}+a_{2})|D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}|^{2}+4a_{2}D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}\frac{w_{t}}{\bar{\eta}}+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\frac{|w_{t}|^{2}}{\bar{\eta}^{2}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=(2a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-(\delta+1)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(2-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)D_{\bar{\eta}}wD_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}\,\mathrm{d}r

(2.37)

\displaystyle\quad+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big((1-\delta)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}-2\delta\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}}\frac{w_{t}}{\bar{\eta}}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-\delta D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(1-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)\Big(\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}}D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}+D_{\bar{\eta}}w\frac{w_{t}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{1}(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\big(g_{t}-g\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\big)\frac{2w_{t}}{\bar{\eta}}+\big(h_{t}-hD_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}\big)D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r.

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps.

Step 1.
First, by Lemma
A.2
, for given
w_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
, we see that there exists a smooth sequence
\{w_{0}^{\vartheta}\}_{\vartheta>0}\subset C^{\infty}(\bar{I})\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
satisfying

(2.38)

w^{\vartheta}_{0}\to w_{0}\qquad\text{in $\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}$ \ as $\vartheta\to 0$}.

Step 2.
Based on this smooth initial data
w_{0}^{\vartheta}
and the Galerkin scheme presented in Step 1 of the proof of Proposition
2.1
, we can construct a Galerkin approximate sequence
w^{N,\vartheta}\in AC([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
and an ODE problem, which are the same as those given in (
2.15
)–(
2.17
).

Now, multiplying
\eqref{galerkin-n}_{1}
by
(\mu_{j}^{N,\vartheta})_{t}(t)
and summing the
resulting equality with respect to
j
from
1
to
N
implies

(2.39)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}L_{1}+\int_{0}^{1}r^{2}\rho_{0}|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r=L_{3}+L_{4}+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}L_{5},

where
(L_{1},X,Y)
are defined as in (
2.20
) and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L_{3}&:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big((2a_{1}+a_{2})\big(-(\delta+1)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(2-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\big)X^{2}\\
&\qquad\qquad+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\big((1-\delta)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}-2\delta\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\big)Y^{2}+2a_{2}\big(-\delta D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(1-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\big)XY\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L_{4}&:=\int_{0}^{1}(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(2\big(g\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}-g_{t}\big)Y+(hD_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}-h_{t})X\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r,\\
L_{5}&:=\int_{0}^{1}(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(2gY+hX\big)\,\mathrm{d}r.\end{aligned}

To estimate
L_{3}
–
L_{4}
, we obtain from (
2.6
) and the Hölder and Young inequalities that

\displaystyle L_{3}

\displaystyle\leq C|\mathscr{D}_{r}\bar{U}|_{\infty}|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}^{2}\leq C(T)|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}^{2},

\displaystyle L_{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(|\mathscr{D}_{r}\bar{U}|_{\infty}|(g,h)|_{2}+|(g_{t},h_{t})|_{2}\big)|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\big(|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}^{2}+|(g,h,g_{t},h_{t})|_{2}^{2}\big).

While for
L_{5}
, we have

|L_{5}|\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}|(g,h)|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}^{2}\qquad\text{for all $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$}.

Thus, by choosing
\varepsilon
small enough, we obtain from (
2.39
), the estimates of
L_{3}
–
L_{5}
, the estimate of
L_{1}
(see (
2.21
)), and the Grönwall inequality that

\displaystyle\|w^{N,\vartheta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})}+\|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})}

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\Big(\|w^{N,\vartheta}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}+\|(g,h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{2})}+\|(g_{t},h_{t})\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}\Big).

For the initial data, it follows from (iii) of Lemma
2.2
, (
2.38
), and the argument similar to (
2.25
)–(
2.26
) that there exist
\vartheta_{0}>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(\vartheta_{0})\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that

\|w^{N,\vartheta}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}\leq 2\|w_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}\qquad\text{for any $\vartheta\in(0,\vartheta_{0}]$ and $N\geq N_{0}$},

which thus leads to the uniform estimate:

(2.40)

\displaystyle\|w^{N,\vartheta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})}+\|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})}

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\Big(\|w^{N,\vartheta}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}+\|(g,h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{2})}+\|(g_{t},h_{t})\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}\Big).

Step 3.
Note that, thanks to the regularity assumptions of
(g,h)
and
(g_{t},h_{t})
in Proposition
2.2
, we have
\mathfrak{B}(t)\in C^{1}[0,T]
and
\mathfrak{c}(t)\in H^{1}(0,T)
, where
(\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{c})(t)
are defined in (
2.18
). Then the classical theory of ODEs (
cf.

[
5
]
) implies that
(\mu^{N,\vartheta})_{t}(t)\in AC([0,T])
, so that
w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}
is differentiable
a.e.
in
t
. Hence, applying
\partial_{t}
to
\eqref{galerkin-n}_{1}
gives

\displaystyle\ \big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{tt}^{N,\vartheta},\xi_{j}\big>+\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta},(2a_{1}+a_{2})D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\frac{w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}}\frac{\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle\quad+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta},\frac{w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}+D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}\frac{\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle\quad+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-(\delta+1)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(2-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta},D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}\Big>

\displaystyle\quad+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big((1-\delta)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}-2\delta\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle\quad+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-\delta D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(1-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big),\frac{w^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}+D_{\bar{\eta}}w^{N,\vartheta}\frac{\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle=\Big<(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(g_{t}-g\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\big),\frac{2\xi_{j}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>+\big<(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(h_{t}-hD_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}\big),D_{\bar{\eta}}\xi_{j}\big>.

Next, multiplying the above by
(\mu_{j}^{N,\vartheta})_{t}
and summing
j
from
1
to
N
, we have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{0}^{1}r^{2}\rho_{0}|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r+L_{6}=L_{7}+L_{8},

where
(X,Y)
are defined as in (
2.20
) and

\displaystyle L_{6}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\big((2a_{1}+a_{2})\widetilde{X}^{2}+4a_{2}\widetilde{X}\widetilde{Y}+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\widetilde{Y}^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}r,

\displaystyle L_{7}

\displaystyle:=(2a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-(\delta+1)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(2-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)X\widetilde{X}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big((1-\delta)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}-2\delta\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)Y\widetilde{Y}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+2a_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-\delta D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(1-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)\big(Y\widetilde{X}+X\widetilde{Y}\big)\,\mathrm{d}r,

\displaystyle L_{8}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\big(g_{t}-g\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\big)2\widetilde{Y}+\big(h_{t}-hD_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}\big)\widetilde{X}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r,\quad(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}):=(D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}^{N,\vartheta},\frac{w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}}{\bar{\eta}}).

For
L_{6}
–
L_{8}
, it follow from (
2.6
),
a_{1}>0
, and
2a_{1}+3a_{2}>0
that

\displaystyle L_{6}

\displaystyle\geq c_{*}|\mathscr{D}_{r}w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}^{2},\qquad L_{7}\leq C|\mathscr{D}_{r}\bar{U}|_{\infty}|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}|\mathscr{D}_{r}w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}},

\displaystyle L_{8}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(|\mathscr{D}_{r}\bar{U}|_{\infty}|(g,h)|_{2}+|(g_{t},h_{t})|_{2}\big)|\mathscr{D}_{r}w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}.

Thus, it follows from the above estimates, an estimate similar to (
2.23
):

|w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}\leq C\big(|w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}+|\mathscr{D}_{r}w_{t}^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}\big)

and the Young inequality that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}+c_{*}\|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}\leq C(T)\big(|\mathscr{D}_{r}w^{N,\vartheta}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}^{2}+|(g,h,g_{t},h_{t})|_{2}^{2}+|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}\big).

Multiplying the above by
t
and integrating over
[\tau,t]
, together with (
2.40
), we have

(2.41)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{t}w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})}^{2}+\int_{\tau}^{t}s\|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\tau|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}(\tau)|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}+C(T)\Big(\|w_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}+\|(g,h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|(g_{t},h_{t})\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}^{2}\Big).

Finally, due to Lemma
A.7
and the fact that
(r^{2}\rho_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2})
, there exists a sequence
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset[0,T]
such that

\tau_{k}\to 0,\quad\tau_{k}|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}(\tau_{k})|_{2,r^{2}\rho_{0}}^{2}\to 0\qquad\ \ \text{as $k\to\infty$}.

Hence, letting
\tau=\tau_{k}\to 0
in (
2.41
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{t}w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(r^{2}\rho_{0})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{s}\|w^{N,\vartheta}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\Big(\|w_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}^{2}+\|(g,h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|(g_{t},h_{t})\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}^{2}\Big).

Step 4. Weak formulation and energy equality.
First, the regularity property (
2.35
) can be derived via a weak convergence argument similar to Step 3 of the proof of Proposition
2.1
. We omit the details here for brevity.

Next, we prove that
\sqrt{t}r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{tt}\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}})
. Since
w_{t}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}})
, we see from (
2.10
) that, for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}
,

\displaystyle\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t},\varphi\big>+(2a_{1}+a_{2})\big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\big>+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\Big>+2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}D_{\bar{\eta}}w,\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle=\Big<g,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>+\big<h,(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\big>.

Differentiating the above with respect to
t
gives that, for
a.e.

t>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big<(r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t}),\varphi\big>

\displaystyle=-\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta},(2a_{1}+a_{2})D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi+4(a_{1}+a_{2})\frac{w_{t}}{\bar{\eta}}\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

\displaystyle\quad-2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta},\frac{w_{t}}{\bar{\eta}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi+D_{\bar{\eta}}w_{t}\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>

(2.42)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\quad-(2a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-(\delta+1)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(2-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)D_{\bar{\eta}}w,D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\Big>\\
&\quad-4(a_{1}+a_{2})\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big((1-\delta)D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}-2\delta\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big)\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}},\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\quad-2a_{2}\Big<\bar{\eta}^{2}\bar{\eta}_{r}\bar{\varrho}^{\delta}\Big(-\delta D_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}+(1-2\delta)\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\Big),\frac{w}{\bar{\eta}}D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi+D_{\bar{\eta}}w\frac{\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>\\
&\quad+\Big<(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(g_{t}-g\frac{\bar{U}}{\bar{\eta}}\big),\frac{2\varphi}{\bar{\eta}}\Big>+\big<(r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(h_{t}-hD_{\bar{\eta}}\bar{U}\big),D_{\bar{\eta}}\varphi\big>,\end{aligned}

which yields

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big<r^{2}\rho_{0}w_{t},\varphi\big>\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\big(|(g_{t},h_{t})|_{2}+|\mathscr{D}_{r}\bar{U}|_{\infty}|(g,h)|_{2}\big)\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{r^{2}\rho_{0}^{\delta}}}
